canonical FVLT instance $\mathrm{Div}(720720) \cong \mathrm{Div}(720) \times \mathrm{Div}(1001)$ with $30 \cdot 8 = 240$ nodes. **Rmk 1.6a** (unconditional): three operationally distinct factorization regimes — *cold* / *warm* / *walk-relative*. **Rmk 1.6b** (interpretive): three distinct levels of primitive object — node / visit / transition. **§16 Key Theorems Reference**: four new entries (1.5b, 2.2e, 1.6a, 1.6b). **New external authority**: [Bir40] (Birkhoff, *Lattice Theory*, 1940). One renumbering pass during kernel integration (3.5b/9.4a/9.4d → 2.2e/1.6a/1.6b). See detailed v2.7.9 → v2.7.10 entry below.)
**Previous:** 2026-05-06 (v2.7.7 / v2.7.8 / v2.7.9 sessions: substrate-layer carrier/lattice/identity work surfaced through Universal Lattice Trainer v2 / v2.13 development (Robin + Claude, May 5 2026); audited and refined by Chris/ChatGPT. **v2.7.7** extended Rmk 9.3e with a zero-clause closing paragraph: 0 belongs to the signed affine / additive carrier but is not a node of any positive divisor lattice; primeFactors(0) is undefined in the finite divisor-lattice sense; landing at or leaving 0 is a carrier event, not a Hasse move. Identity symmetry stated explicitly: 1 is the multiplicative root of every Div(N); 0 is the additive hub of the carrier layer. **v2.7.8** added Rmk 9.3f (Operational Transition Typology on the Signed Substrate, definitional): atom (sign-preserving Hasse cover, single prime factor difference), jump (polynomial-expression-required transition between nonzero values), zero (carrier event with $n = 0$ or $n' = 0$); test order is zero first, atom second, jump residual; "Operational" keystone in title is a guardrail — the remark is implementation vocabulary made explicit, not a mathematical partition theorem about all maps on $\mathbb{Z}$. Sign-changing targets may be routed via $\pm 1 \to 0 \to m'$ when the instrument elects to separate multiplicative descent from additive launch; direct AP sign-changing jumps remain valid carrier jumps when explicitly declared. **v2.7.9** added three interpretive entries clarifying the difference/slope register distinction and the unit floor: Rmk 2.2a.1 (Difference–Slope Duality) — forward differences live in the difference register where no denominator has yet been introduced; standard polynomial coefficients pick up factorial denominators when the same discrete motion is translated into the slope/calculus register; binomial basis $\binom{x}{k}$ makes the toll explicit (falling factorial $(x)_k$ as numerator, $k!$ as denominator); factorial filtration $N_k = k!$ measures the basis-change cost between the fraction-free lattice register and the slope-normalized calculus register. Load-bearing sentence: *"the denominator is not missing from the lattice register; it has not yet been introduced."* Guardrail (carrier-usage independence): factorial denominator is a basis-change cost, NOT a register-crossing diagnostic; carrier usage is detected by the atom/jump/zero typology of Rmk 9.3f, not by the standard-form denominator. Two worked counterexamples settle this: pure-Hasse-atomic walk $[12, 4, 2, 1]$ has standard form $(1/6)(-5x^3 + 33x^2 - 76x + 72)$ — denominator 6 despite zero carrier crossings; $[6, 12, 30, 60]$ contains a non-atomic jump (12→30, ratio 5/2) yet standardizes to $6x^2 + 6$ — denominator 1 via algebraic cancellation. Note 2.2a.2 (Lattice Metric ↔ Polynomial Degree, Descent-through-Zero Walks): for walks $n \to \text{atom}^* 1 \to \text{zero } 0 \to \text{jump } m$ where atomic descent is a monotone shortest path through Hasse diagram, visit count is $\Omega(n) + 3$, Newton interpolant has degree at most $\Omega(n) + 2$, and standard-form denominator divides $(\Omega(n) + 2)!$. Identifies lattice metric $d(n, 1) = \Omega(n)$ with polynomial signature degree of descent-through-zero walks (modulo +2 offset). Two senses of shell distinguished: lattice shell (Div(720) hosting nodes up to $\Omega = 7$) vs polynomial shell (factorial filtration rung $N_k = k!$ that the descent-through-zero walk's signature requires to standardize). Rmk 9.3g (The Unit Floor and Fraction Boundary): names 1 as floor of multiplicative descent in two senses simultaneously — multiplicative identity (root of every Div(N), terminus of any monotone $\div p$ Hasse descent) and register boundary (lower bound of integer divisor lattice; below 1, continued division produces fractions, exiting Div(N) into the slope register). Three-stack attribution recorded in kernel changelog comments. **§16 Key Theorems Reference (v2.7.9 only)**: three new entries added (2.2a.1, 2.2a.2, 9.3g) as short pointers; first §9.x entries in the reference table. Companion artifact: Universal Lattice Trainer v2.13 (Robin, May 2026) implementing Newton Signature v1 — measurement-only readout of an already-produced walk that fits the minimum-degree Newton interpolant. **No new external authorities, no new Open Problems, no theorem changes** across v2.7.7–v2.7.9; substrate-layer clarifications and one new definitional remark (9.3f) only.)

**Previous:** 2026-05-04 (v2.7.6 session: §9.3a Rmk 9.3d (Architectural Content of the Residue Fan, interpretive) added, with internal refinement pass (provenance/naming) applied within the same version. Names the selection rule under which the residue-class affine fan $y = Nx + b$ reads as basis/complement architecture rather than mere modular arithmetic: (i) $|B(N)| \geq 2$ — multi-prime basis, so the fan's non-unit sector has internal collapse; (ii) $N+1$ supported on $B'(N)$ — automatic from $\gcd(N, N+1) = 1$, but the strength of the architectural reading depends on whether $N+1$ is a single complement prime, complement-product, or includes far-complement primes. Minimal small-modulus examples are $N \in \{6, 10, 12\}$ with single-prime complement openings ($6+1 = 7$, $10+1 = 11$, $12+1 = 13$); the factorial shell $N_0 = 720$ is the strengthened form via Theorem 3.7 ($719 \equiv -1$ and $721 \equiv +1 \pmod{d}$ for every $d \mid 720$, $d > 1$) plus the canonical near-complement support of $721 = 7 \cdot 103$. Connects condition (i) to Theorem 8.20h.3: the $|B| \geq 2$ geometric prerequisite is what activates the basis collapse; the $r \geq 3$ topological prerequisite further requires nontrivial loop topology in the proper-complement order complex; both hold at $N_0 = 720$. Architectural claim is interpretive; underlying arithmetic claims are unconditional. **Provenance.** The residue-ray scaffolding — residue classes drawn as rays from a center — belongs to the classical modular-arithmetic and arithmetic-progression tradition, not to FVLT. The primitive arms ($\gcd(b, N) = 1$) are the reduced residue classes modulo $N$, i.e. the arithmetic progressions appearing in Dirichlet's theorem on primes in APs. The FVLT contribution is the architectural reading: the basis/complement split, the K(N) coprime spectrum as a structural object, and the selection rule (i) + (ii) under which the classical fan reads as basis/complement architecture. Robin's 2018-19 hand-drawn residue-class ray diagram (mod 12, with explicit equation $y = \mathrm{mod} \cdot x + b$, prime/composite labels per ray, basis collapse in the central region, complement primes on the unit arms) records her working-through of the classical scaffolding on the way to the FVLT architectural reading. Cartesian-fan progression as Robin's working notes: 2018-19 hand-drawn → 2019 GeoGebra (hypatiaa, mod 12 residue rays) → 2022 cartesia2 (complement prime 7 identified as natural denominator — first FVLT-specific reading) → 2024 GeoGebra "Factorial Oracle" (gearbox formula $k_1 = N - 1$, $k_2 = N + 1$ — trilattice in pre-formal form) → kernel v2.6.1 §9.1a / Lemma 9.3a (formal helix family + no-intersection lemma) → kernel v2.7.4 Rmk 9.3c (substrate-level $\varphi$ on $\Gamma_5$) → kernel v2.7.6 Rmk 9.3d (selection rule for architectural content). Three-stack on the v2.7.6 commit + refinement: Claude (over-stated "first prime past N is on b=1" pattern initially; accepted Robin's correction to "$N + 1$ on b=1 by gcd identity"; accepted Robin's further correction excluding $|B| = 1$ moduli; formalized Rmk 9.3d as kernel insertion; produced audit brief for Chris on the provenance/naming refinement; applied refinement under Chris's clearance), Chris/ChatGPT (audit of the refinement: cleared the patch, recommended "reduced residue classes modulo N" as the precise classical noun rather than "Dirichlet arithmetic progressions" used as a noun, Dirichlet's theorem mentioned but not cited as separate reference [Dir1837], confirmed no version bump needed, flagged that classical scaffolding vs. FVLT architectural reading is the right threading), Robin (uploaded the 2018-19 diagram; provided two corrections during the analysis [durable statement is about $N+1$ not "first prime past N"; selection rule excludes $|B| = 1$ moduli]; drafted the Rmk 9.3d text; assigned interpretive/unconditional status; identified §9 as the kernel slot; provided the over-attribution correction "the ancestor is older than me" and the technical naming "the residue classes are also dirichlet arithmetic progressions"). No new external authorities. No new Open Problems. No theorem changes. No annotation register changes. No §16 changes.)

## Document Summary

*Framework: Ratioque FVLT v3.2.0 semantic-correction baseline. Active architecture is atlas-general: $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}=\{\mathrm{Div}(N):N>0\}$, with Vernier harmonic instruments, exact-conductor signal analysis, finite Hasse/crystal realizations, and factorial-shell worked instances.*
*Principle: The structure is the algorithm; program comments and UI labels are implementation evidence only.*
*Basis: Atomic positional objects are $\mathrm{Div}(N)$ across positive integers. The $720=6!$ / $|S_6|=720$ material remains a named factorial-shell and $1001$ complement worked instance, not a universal substrate or atom.*
*Transform: Amplitude exponent $\alpha$ and phasor-side ALG-1/2/3 readouts remain routed as implementation-supported instruments, with theorem status inherited from `kernel_v3_2_5.md`.*
*Analysis: $E_q^{\mathrm{line}}(x)=|\langle x,c_q\rangle|^2/\|c_q\|^2$ is the scalar Ramanujan-line/coherent statistic; $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x)=\|P_qx\|^2$ is full exact-conductor subspace energy. Parseval/Plancherel belongs to $E_q^{\mathrm{full}}$; energy-only output loses phase and does not reconstruct a general signal.*
*Windows: Windowed forms $E_{q,W}^{\mathrm{line}}$ and $E_{q,W}^{\mathrm{full}}$ require declared support, absolute versus reanchored indexing, normalization, and taper policy. Universal boundary-independence and window-size-insensitivity are not active theorem claims.*
*Vernier: The finite gearbox contact rule is $n_3 \mid t_2s$; divisor recovery $\mathrm{Div}(s)$ is the restricted reference specialization $t_2=1$.*
*Hilbert registers: $H_{\mathrm{Hasse}}(N)=\ell^2(\mathrm{Div}(N))\cong\bigotimes_i\mathbb{C}^{a_i+1}$ and $H_{\mathrm{harm}}(N)=\ell^2(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{C}^N=\bigoplus_{q\mid N}V_q$ are distinct. At $N=720$, the dimensions are $30$ and $720$. Shared divisor labels do not imply a canonical unitary map.*
*ASCII aliases: `H_Hasse(N)` and `H_harm(N)` name those two Hilbert registers in release notes and validators.*
*Bridge: The valid bridge from harmonic signals to divisor-indexed output is measurement data such as $x\mapsto(E_q^{\mathrm{full}}(x))_{q\mid N}$, generally nonlinear and phase-losing.*
*720/1001 instance: Retained facts include the 720 selection theorem under its named criteria, $720/1001$ bridge material, $\mathrm{Div}(720720)\cong\mathrm{Div}(720)\times\mathrm{Div}(1001)$, graph invariants, spectral examples, and historical UI/instrument presets.*
*Research branch: The old Virasoro/Fock/Stone-von-Neumann thread is preserved as non-normative research/history. It is not active theorem authority and is not a release dependency.*
*Status: CC29 and OP30 remain open/out-of-kernel as previously recorded. Claims not validated in the supplied workpack are marked research proposal, historical statement, superseded as stated, implementation evidence, or verification unavailable.*

## Signal Processing

**[V14a]** P.P. Vaidyanathan & S. Tenneti, "Ramanujan Sums in the Context of Signal Processing — Part I: Fundamentals," *IEEE Trans. Signal Process.*, vol. 62, no. 16, pp. 4145–4157, 2014.
- Cited: §1.4 (Remark 1.2), §5.5, Attribution Note
- Role: Ramanujan subspace framework for signal decomposition. The FVLT builds on this by organizing subspaces via the divisor lattice rather than a flat list.

**[V14b]** P.P. Vaidyanathan & S. Tenneti, "Ramanujan Sums in the Context of Signal Processing — Part II: FIR Representations and Applications," *IEEE Trans. Signal Process.*, vol. 62, no. 16, pp. 4158–4172, 2014.
- Cited: §1.4 (Remark 1.2), §5.5, Attribution Note
- Role: FIR filter representations via Ramanujan sums; companion to [V14a].

**[V20]** P.P. Vaidyanathan, "Srinivasa Ramanujan and signal-processing problems," *Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A*, vol. 378, 20180446, 2020.
- Cited: Remark 1.1
- Role: Survey connecting Ramanujan's number theory to modern signal processing.

**[P09]** M. Planat, "Ramanujan sums analysis of long-period sequences and 1/f noise," *Europhys. Lett.*, vol. 85, 40005, 2009.
- Cited: Remark 1.1, §1.4 (Remark 1.2), Attribution Note
- Role: Earlier application of Ramanujan sums to time series; treats divisors as flat frequency list without lattice structure.

---

## Number Theory — Classical

**[Ab24]** N.H. Abel, "Mémoire sur les équations algébriques, où on démontre l'impossibilité de la résolution de l'équation générale du cinquième degré," 1824; published in *Œuvres complètes*, vol. 1, 1881.
- Cited: Historical context for Remark 8.18b (Radical-Free Architecture)
- Role: Proof that the general quintic cannot be solved by radicals. The FVLT's radical-free architecture sidesteps this obstruction by operating through exponential sums rather than radical extraction. Abel's result is relevant context — the framework works in a different algebraic category — but does not directly explain the bridge closure at S₆.

**[Gal32]** É. Galois, "Mémoire sur les conditions de résolubilité des équations par radicaux," written 1831, published posthumously in *J. Math. Pures Appl.*, vol. 11, pp. 417–433, 1846.
- Cited: Historical context for Remark 8.18b (Radical-Free Architecture)
- Role: Galois theory: a polynomial is solvable by radicals iff its Galois group is solvable. Sₚ is solvable iff p ≤ 4; Aₚ is simple for p ≥ 5. **Correction (v1.7+):** An earlier version of Theorem 8.14b claimed B = {2,3,5} spans "exactly the solvable primes." This was false — S₅ is not solvable (A₅ is simple). The bridge closure at S₆ is an arithmetic fact about 5-smooth totients, not a consequence of Galois solvability. Abel and Galois provide algebraic context for why the FVLT's exponential-modular architecture avoids radical extraction, but they do not explain the bridge identity.

**[R18]** S. Ramanujan, "On certain trigonometrical sums and their applications in the theory of numbers," *Trans. Cambridge Philos. Soc.*, vol. XXII, no. 13, pp. 259–276, 1918.
- Cited: §5 (transform definition), Theorem 8.19 (CRT factorization), throughout
- Role: Defines the Ramanujan sum c_q(n). The fundamental basis function of the FVLT.

**[R15]** S. Ramanujan, "Highly composite numbers," *Proc. London Math. Soc.*, ser. 2, vol. 14, pp. 347–409, 1915.
- Cited: Definition 8.5d (Shell Selection)
- Role: Highly composite numbers — integers with more divisors than any smaller positive integer. N₀ = 720 is highly composite.

**[HW08]** G.H. Hardy & E.M. Wright, *An Introduction to the Theory of Numbers*, 6th ed., Oxford University Press, 2008.
- Cited: §9 (Farey sequences §3), §5 (Ramanujan sums §16.6), §10 (Stern-Brocot tree §10)
- Role: Standard reference for the number-theoretic foundations.

**[Ste58]** M.A. Stern, "Über eine zahlentheoretische Funktion," *J. reine angew. Math.*, vol. 55, pp. 193–220, 1858.
- Cited: §10.3 (Stern-Brocot Tree Navigation), Attribution Note
- Role: Original Stern-Brocot construction.

**[Bro61]** A. Brocot, "Calcul des rouages par approximation, nouvelle méthode," *Revue Chronométrique*, vol. 3, pp. 186–194, 1861.
- Cited: §10.3, Attribution Note
- Role: Independent construction of Stern-Brocot tree for gear ratios.

**[For38]** L.R. Ford, "Fractions," *Amer. Math. Monthly*, vol. 45, no. 9, pp. 586–601, 1938.
- Cited: §9 (Farey Spine)
- Role: Ford circles; geometric realization of Farey fractions.

**[Che73]** J.R. Chen, "On the representation of a large even integer as the sum of a prime and the product of at most two primes," *Sci. Sinica*, vol. 16, pp. 157–176, 1973.
- Cited: §6 (Goldbach resonance context)
- Role: Chen's theorem — the strongest unconditional result toward Goldbach.

**[SylF]** J.J. Sylvester, "On subinvariants, i.e. semi-invariants to binary quantics of an unlimited order," *Amer. J. Math.*, vol. 5, pp. 79–136, 1882.
- Cited: Remark 8.25 (Sylvester Wave Connection)
- Role: Sylvester-Frobenius coin problem; wave decomposition of restricted partition functions. The 8 Sylvester waves at divisors of 1001 correspond to the 8 E_q layers of the dual spectrum.

## Number Theory — Contemporary

**[GM24]** L. Guth & J. Maynard, "New large value estimates for Dirichlet polynomials," *arXiv:2405.20552*, 2024.
- Cited: Open Problem 5, Attribution Note
- Role: Zero density estimates for ζ(s) via incidence geometry. The trilinear decomposition into smooth + spikes is structurally parallel to E_q partitioned at primorial boundaries. Catalyst for the E_q measurement layer.

**[May15]** J. Maynard, "Small gaps between primes," *Ann. of Math.*, vol. 181, no. 1, pp. 383–413, 2015.
- Cited: Context for prime gap structure
- Role: Bounded prime gaps; relevant to coprime shadow discussion (§8.4).

- Cited: Context for prime gap structure
- Role: First proof of bounded gaps between primes.

## Prime Spirals and Integer-Address Visualizations

### Formal (integer-spiral geometry; prime density along paths)

**[SUW64]** M. L. Stein, S. M. Ulam, M. B. Wells, "A Visual Display of Some Properties of the Distribution of Primes," *Amer. Math. Monthly*, vol. 71, no. 5, pp. 516–520, 1964. doi:10.2307/2312588.
- Role: The Ulam spiral — integers on a square grid in spiral order, primes marked; prime-rich
  diagonals/axes from quadratic sequences. Prior art for integer-address geometry and
  prime-density-along-paths, NOT for the divisor-lattice embedding (see §4B).

**[Sacks94]** R. Sacks, the Sacks (number) spiral, 1994. Independent / web work (numberspiral.com); no formal publication. **(informal)**
- Role: Archimedean-spiral variant of Ulam (one square per rotation); polynomial sequences become
  rotational curves. The **closer visual grammar** to the FVLT rosette/cone — rotational integer
  addressing rather than square grid. FVLT's vernier phase k·s·π/N is itself a rotational advance per
  additive step. Still an additive-address plot of the integer, not a divisor-lattice opening.

**[HL23]** G. H. Hardy, J. E. Littlewood, "Some Problems of 'Partitio Numerorum'; III: On the Expression of a Number as a Sum of Primes," *Acta Mathematica*, vol. 44, pp. 1–70, 1923. doi:10.1007/BF02403921.
- Role: Contains "Conjecture F" — the asymptotic prime density of a quadratic ax²+bx+c, governed by
  the local-obstruction product A = ∏_p (p − ω(p))/(p − 1), ω(p) = roots mod p. The local-density
  resonance with FVLT's modular-contact reading is a resemblance of kind, not object (see §4B).

**[BH62]** P. T. Bateman, R. A. Horn, "A heuristic asymptotic formula concerning the distribution of prime numbers," *Math. Comp.*, vol. 16, no. 79, pp. 363–367, 1962. doi:10.1090/S0025-5718-1962-0148632-7.
- Role: The general framework subsuming Hardy–Littlewood Conjecture F — prime frequency among the
  values of polynomial systems via local-factor products. The analytic backbone of the prime-density
  layer of this flank.

**[AFG20]** S. L. Aletheia-Zomlefer, L. Fukshansky, S. R. Garcia, "The Bateman–Horn Conjecture: Heuristics, History, and Applications," *Expo. Math.*, vol. 38, no. 4, pp. 430–479, 2020. (arXiv:1807.08899)
- Role: Modern survey tying Bateman–Horn, Hardy–Littlewood, prime-generating polynomials, and Ulam's
  spiral together; the single best anchor for this flank. (Cross-link: Garcia co-authors the
  geometric-flank graphic-nature program too.)

### Informal (divisor-function values colored on integer spirals)

**[Col]** J.-F. Colonna (École Polytechnique), generalized Ulam spiral with integers sized/colored by their number of divisors, 1980s (commonly cited as 1988). lactamme.polytechnique.fr.
- Role: Named precursor for divisor-COUNT coloring on the spiral (sphere radius ∝ √(number of
  divisors); primes white) — a divisor SCALAR per integer, not the lattice. Attribution verified;
  Colonna's own page dates the idea to "the 1980s."

**[DivPlot]** J. Ventrella, "Divisor Drips and Square Root Waves" / The Divisor Plot, divisorplot.com (ventrella.com).
- Role: Composites on the number spiral shaded by d(x), primes left as the white holes; "product
  curves" (squares, pronics). Closest in spirit — the divisor-first inversion ("primes are holes in
  composite patterns") — and so the boundary matters most here. Still a scalar shading, not a lattice.
  Attribution verified; Ventrella credits Sacks's number spiral as the influence.

**[GNS]** J. H. Williamson (University of Glasgow), "Number Spirals," dcs.gla.ac.uk/~jhw (johnhw.github.io), c. 2009.
- Role: Extends Ulam/Sacks (and the Vogel spiral) to color by the count of distinct prime factors
  ω(n). Divisor scalar. Attribution verified.

**[Smooth]** "The Smoothness Spiral," Modulo Errors blog (maths.straylight.co.uk). **(informal/web; author not identified on the page — retained as an unattributed web reference)**
- Role: Sacks-layout Archimedean spiral colored by smoothness / largest prime factor. Divisor scalar.

**[Wolf]** Wolfram MathWorld, "Prime Spiral"; Wolfram Language DivisorSigma documentation.
- Role: τ(n), σ(n), and the mean of an integer's divisors plotted on the Ulam spiral. Divisor scalars.

## Combinatorial Topology

**[Rot64]** G.-C. Rota, "On the foundations of combinatorial theory I. Theory of Möbius functions," *Z. Wahrscheinlichkeitstheorie*, vol. 2, pp. 340–368, 1964.
- Cited: Theorem 8.27 (Möbius as Euler Characteristic)
- Role: Möbius function on posets as Euler characteristic of order complexes. The theoretical foundation for the Möbius-Betti Bridge (§8.6).

**[YXC21a]** P.-T. Yen, K. Xia, & S.A. Cheong, "Understanding changes in the topology and geometry of financial market correlations during a market crash," *Entropy*, vol. 23, no. 9, 1211, 2021. doi:10.3390/e23091211.
- Cited: Remark 8.33 (Independent Empirical Confirmation)
- Role: TDA + Ollivier-Ricci curvature on Taiwan stock market crash. Identifies cavitation, rupture, puncture — topological processes independently confirming E_q layer transitions.

**[YC21]** P.-T. Yen & S.A. Cheong, "Using topological data analysis (TDA) and persistent homology to analyze the stock markets in Singapore and Taiwan," *Front. Phys.*, vol. 9, 572216, 2021. doi:10.3389/fphy.2021.572216.
- Cited: Remark 8.33
- Role: Companion paper; persistent homology and Betti number tracking through market phases.

**[Bjö95]** A. Björner, "Topological methods," in *Handbook of Combinatorics*, vol. 2, R.L. Graham, M. Grötschel, & L. Lovász, eds., North-Holland/Elsevier, Amsterdam, 1995, pp. 1819–1872.
- Cited: §8.4b (Theorem 8.20h.1, Verification line)
- Role: Canonical reference for the topology of order complexes of finite posets, including the result that the order complex of the proper Boolean lattice $B_k^\circ = B_k \setminus \{\hat 0, \hat 1\}$ is homotopy equivalent to the (k−2)-sphere. Cited as the external authority for the topology underlying Theorem 8.20h.1. The framework's contribution at r = 3 is not the homotopy type itself but the recognition that this is the unique admissible factorial-complement closure index at which the 1-cycle is the primary topological feature (Remark 8.20h.2) and at which the FVLT bridge dynamics become non-degenerate (Theorem 8.20h.3).

**[Wac07]** M.L. Wachs, "Poset Topology: Tools and Applications," in *Geometric Combinatorics*, IAS/Park City Mathematics Series vol. 13, E. Miller, V. Reiner, & B. Sturmfels, eds., American Mathematical Society, Providence, RI, 2007, pp. 497–615; arXiv:math/0602226.
- Cited: §8.4b (Theorem 8.20h.1, Verification line)
- Role: Lecture notes on poset topology with explicit treatment of the proper Boolean lattice and its order complex. Companion authority to [Bjö95] for the homotopy type $\Delta(B_k^\circ) \simeq S^{k-2}$. Provides the framework-independent verification that the worked example's r = 3 case ($\Delta(B_3^\circ) \simeq S^1$, hexagonal cycle) and r = 2 case ($\Delta(B_2^\circ) \simeq S^0$, two disconnected vertices) are instances of a general classical result rather than framework-internal claims.

**[Epp96]** D. Eppstein, "On the Parity of Graph Spanning Tree Numbers," Technical Report 96-14, Department of Information and Computer Science, University of California, Irvine, 1996. Available: https://ics.uci.edu/~eppstein/pubs/Epp-TR-96-14.pdf
- Cited: Appendix K, Remark K.4d
- Role: Canonical reference for the parity theorem: a graph $G$ has an even number of spanning trees if and only if $G$ contains an Eulerian edge cut. Provides the external authority for step (2) of the status chain in Remark K.4d, which translates the empirical observation $K(720) \not\equiv 0 \pmod 2$ into the structural statement "$G_{720}$ has no Eulerian edge cut." The framework's contribution is the application of Eppstein's theorem to the divisor-lattice Hasse graph $G_{720}$, not the theorem itself.

## Algebra and Representation Theory

**[Bir40]** G. Birkhoff, *Lattice Theory*, American Mathematical Society Colloquium Publications, vol. 25, 1st ed. 1940; 2nd (revised) ed. 1948; 3rd ed. 1967.
- Cited: Rmk 1.5b (Coordinate vs. closure-of-description substrate, v2.7.10); Rmk 1.6b (Three observation contexts, v2.7.10)
- Role: Order-ideal representation theorem for finite distributive lattices. Provides the canonical isomorphism between $\text{Div}(N)$ and a closure-of-description / concept-lattice presentation when the attribute set $\Sigma$ consists of prime-power divisibility predicates over the join-irreducibles of $\text{Div}(N)$. The closure-of-description substrate $\Lambda_{\text{desc}}$ diverges from $\text{Div}(N)$ when observation-derived attributes (walker history, lens state, atom/jump/zero classification) enter $\Sigma$ — this is the operationally interesting regime for the trainer. The work also founds the systematic study of Galois connections in algebra, which Ore (1944) generalized into abstract adjunctions and Wille (1982) restructured into Formal Concept Analysis. The kernel cites Birkhoff specifically rather than the FCA literature: the order-ideal theorem is load-bearing for the canonical-$\Sigma$ recovery, while the FCA framing is reserved for paper-level community positioning.

**[SvN31]** M.H. Stone, "Linear transformations in Hilbert space. III. Operational methods and group theory," *Proc. Nat. Acad. Sci.*, vol. 16, pp. 172–175, 1930; J. von Neumann, "Die Eindeutigkeit der Schrödingerschen Operatoren," *Math. Ann.*, vol. 104, pp. 570–578, 1931.
- Cited: Legacy Theorem 13.6 (Stone-von Neumann at factorial level), now inside the non-normative Sections 12-13 research/historical branch.
- Role: Classical uniqueness theorem for irreducible representations of the Heisenberg algebra. v3.2.0 status: historical/reference background only; no active Stone-von-Neumann theorem is asserted for FVLT.

**[Foc32]** V.A. Fock, "Konfigurationsraum und zweite Quantelung," *Z. Phys.*, vol. 75, pp. 622–647, 1932.
- Cited: Legacy §13 (Fock Space Representation), now inside the non-normative Sections 12-13 research/historical branch.
- Role: Symmetric-algebra/Fock-space background. v3.2.0 status: historical/reference background only; the finite Hasse Hilbert realization is not called a Fock space unless a future one-particle/symmetric-algebra construction is supplied.

**[Bar61]** V. Bargmann, "On a Hilbert space of analytic functions and an associated integral transform," *Comm. Pure Appl. Math.*, vol. 14, pp. 187–214, 1961.
- Cited: Legacy Definition 13.5, Remark 13.7, now inside the non-normative Sections 12-13 research/historical branch.
- Role: Holomorphic realization — states as polynomials in formal variables. v3.2.0 status: research/historical background, not active theorem authority.

**[AT09]** E. Artin & J. Tate, *Class Field Theory*, AMS Chelsea, 2009 (reprinted from 1967 notes).
- Cited: Theorem 14.16
- Role: Artin reciprocity law; character correspondence Gal(ℚᵃᵇ/ℚ) ≅ Ẑ×.

**[FGK14]** C. F. Fowler, S. R. Garcia, G. Karaali, "Ramanujan sums as supercharacters," *Ramanujan Journal*, vol. 35, pp. 205–241, 2014.
- Cited: Remark 5.4a (Supercharacter Classification)
- Role: Proves that Ramanujan sums c_q(n) are supercharacters on cyclic groups ℤ/Nℤ in the Diaconis-Isaacs framework. The partition of ℤ/Nℤ into gcd-classes defines a supercharacter theory whose supercharacters are exactly the Ramanujan sums. Classifies the FVLT's measurement basis in combinatorial representation theory. Garcia and collaborators also developed the **geometry** of cyclic-supercharacter value-images in
the complex plane [DGL15, GHL15, L19, Br13] — the nearest geometric prior art for complex-plane
value-images of exponential sums, demarcated in the originality section (value-image vs embedded
lattice). An art-oriented presentation of the same plots appears in the Bridges series.

**[DGL15]** W. Duke, S. R. Garcia, B. Lutz, "The graphic nature of Gaussian periods," *Proc. Amer. Math. Soc.*, vol. 143, no. 5, pp. 1849–1863, 2015. (arXiv:1212.6825)
- Role: Initiates the visual study of cyclic-supercharacter / Gaussian-period **values** plotted in
  the complex plane, proving envelope-curve bounds (e.g. the m-cusped hypocycloid that bounds the
  image of a cyclic supercharacter of prime-power order). The **nearest geometric prior art** for
  complex-plane value-images of exponential sums: same medium as the §9.7 embedding. FVLT departs in
  *object* — it embeds the divisor lattice itself, not the supercharacter value-image (see §4A).

**[GHL15]** S. R. Garcia, T. Hyde, B. Lutz, "Gauss's hidden menagerie: from cyclotomy to supercharacters," *Notices Amer. Math. Soc.*, vol. 62, no. 8, pp. 878–888, 2015. (arXiv:1501.07507)
- Role: Expository account of the complex-plane geometry of cyclic supercharacters / Gaussian periods
  — the "menagerie" of value-image shapes and their number-theoretic origins. Same geometric medium
  as FVLT; different object (value-image vs embedded lattice).

**[L19]** B. Lutz, "Graphical cyclic supercharacters for composite moduli," *Proc. Amer. Math. Soc.*, vol. 147, no. 9, pp. 3649–3663, 2019. (arXiv:1601.01031)
- Role: Extends the value-image geometry to **composite** n — the regime overlapping FVLT's composite
  shells (e.g. 720). Demarcation sharpest here: Lutz studies the image of σ_X for composite n; FVLT
  embeds Div(N) for composite N. Both composite, different object.

**[Br13]** J. L. Brumbaugh, M. Bulkow, L. A. G. German, S. R. Garcia, M. Michal, A. P. Turner, "The graphic nature of the symmetric group," *Exp. Math.*, vol. 22, no. 4, pp. 421–442, 2013.
- Role: Value-image geometry of exponential sums attached to Sₙ. Touches FVLT's S₆ anchoring at the
  level of the symmetric group — again as value-image rather than lattice embedding.

**[Br14]** J. L. Brumbaugh et al., "Supercharacters, exponential sums, and the uncertainty principle," *J. Number Theory*, vol. 144, pp. 151–175, 2014.
- Role: Develops supercharacters as a **generalized DFT basis** with an associated uncertainty
  principle. The supercharacter-as-Fourier-basis lineage (cf. [FGK14]); relevant to the harmonic
  register, not the geometry.

**[P24]** S. Platt, "Visual aspects of Gaussian periods and analogues," *Int. J. Number Theory*, 2024. (arXiv:2308.05220)
- Role: Recent survey and extension of the value-image program (incl. a class-field-theory view). The
  current state of the geometric prior art.

**[DI08]** P. Diaconis, I. M. Isaacs, "Supercharacters and superclasses for algebra groups," *Trans. Amer. Math. Soc.*, vol. 360, no. 5, pp. 2359–2392, 2008.
- Role: Foundational supercharacter theory underlying [FGK14] and the entire graphic-nature program.
  (Promoted from a prose mention in the [FGK14] role line to a formal entry.)

**[Lan70]** R.P. Langlands, "Problems in the theory of automorphic forms," in *Lectures in Modern Analysis and Applications III*, Springer LNM 170, pp. 18–61, 1970.
- Cited: Remark 14.17, Open Problem 5
- Role: The Langlands program — automorphic forms, Galois representations, L-functions. The FVLT's factorial tower lives adjacent to this program at the level of shared indexing by Ẑ×.

**[Bor92]** R.E. Borcherds, "Monstrous moonshine and monstrous Lie superalgebras," *Invent. Math.*, vol. 109, pp. 405–444, 1992.
- Cited: Open Problem 5 (note on phase transition beyond S₆)
- Role: Proof of the Conway–Norton monstrous moonshine conjecture, establishing that the Fourier coefficients of the j-function are dimensions of graded representations of the Monster group. The Monster's connection to modular forms via moonshine demonstrates that extraordinary arithmetic structure exists beyond the bridge closure at S₆. If the FVLT's factorial depth connects to Langlands level structure, moonshine may represent the continuation of the same indexing past the closed regime.

## Differential Geometry and Physics

**[Has30]** H. Hasse, "Über die Darstellbarkeit von Zahlen durch quadratische Formen im Körper der rationalen Zahlen," *J. reine angew. Math.*, vol. 152, pp. 129–148, 1924. See also: H. Hasse, *Höhere Algebra*, vols. I–II, Sammlung Göschen, 1926–1927. The Hasse diagram as a visualization of partially ordered sets became standard in the 1930s through Hasse's textbooks and lectures.
- Cited: §1.4 (The Hasse Instrument), §8.7 (Spinor-Tensor Decomposition)
- Role: The Hasse diagram. FVLT's novelty claim (§1.4) is the use of this diagram as a measurement instrument rather than a descriptive illustration.

**[Dir28]** P.A.M. Dirac, "The Quantum Theory of the Electron," *Proc. R. Soc. Lond. A*, vol. 117, no. 778, pp. 610–624, 1928.
- Cited: Remark 4.6a (Clinamen as Spin Connection), Proposition 8.39 (Double Cover), §8.7
- Role: The Dirac equation, spinor fields, chirality (Weyl decomposition), and the double cover SU(2) → SO(3). The framework's helix-chirality-clinamen primitives produce Dirac spinor geometry. The "belt trick" (720° = 4π restoration period) maps to the 360→720 covering edge in Div(720).

**[Car13]** É. Cartan, "Les groupes projectifs qui ne laissent invariante aucune multiplicité plane," *Bull. Soc. Math. France*, vol. 41, pp. 53–96, 1913. See also: É. Cartan, "Sur une classe remarquable d'espaces de Riemann," *Bull. Soc. Math. France*, vol. 54, pp. 214–264, 1926.
- Cited: Remark 4.6a (Clinamen and Connection-Like Behavior)
- Role: Spin connections in differential geometry. Cartan's connection is a Lie algebra-valued 1-form on a principal bundle over a smooth manifold — formal machinery the discrete factorial lattice does not possess. The clinamen G(σ) = ln(1+κσ) exhibits the same *behavioral profile* (zero → trivial, nonzero → coupling) but is a scalar on a discrete lattice, not a connection in Cartan's sense. Whether the factorial lattice admits a bundle structure making G a genuine connection is open. Cartan provides the continuum framework against which the discrete behavioral analogy is measured.

## Philosophy

**[Epi]** Epicurus, *Letter to Herodotus*, c. 300 BCE; Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura*, c. 55 BCE.
- Cited: Definition 4.7 (Clinamen), Remark 4.6a (Epicurean-Spinor Dictionary), throughout
- Role: The clinamen — the irreducible swerve — is the philosophical foundation of the framework. Lucretius identified the geometric prerequisite for structure: irreducible deviation from parallel motion. The helix construction that produces spinor geometry — double cover, chirality, k₁/k₂ gearing — is original to FVLT (Miller). Dirac's equation confirms the output. The chain is: Lucretius (prerequisite: irreducible swerve) → Miller (construction: helix → spinor) → Dirac (validation). The framework's epistemological principles (perception over prediction, description over prescription, humility over certainty) derive from Epicurean empiricism.

## Biology

**[Mar70]** L. Margulis, *Origin of Eukaryotic Cells*, Yale University Press, 1970.
- Cited: §14.2b (Biological-Algebraic Isomorphism), Remark 14.10
- Role: Endosymbiotic origin of mitochondria. The FVLT generates the viral-mitochondrial distinction *internally* from projection coherence, CRT factorizability, and generator retention (§14.2b). Margulis is cited not as a source of structure but as **independent validation**: biology arrived at the same architecture — persistent substructure, partial autonomy, non-parasitic replication, long-term coherence under embedding — through 2 billion years of evolutionary pressure. The citation confirms that the formalism's structural distinctions are real, following the Epicurean methodology: the mathematics describes what is structurally possible; nature is then consulted to see whether it agrees.

## Worked Examples and Internal Audit Notes

*This section catalogs internal FVLT computations that support specific kernel propositions. Entries here are not external authorities; they are explicit verifications carried out within the framework, archived with their reproducible computational basis. Each entry cross-references the proposition it supports and the script that reproduces the computation. External authorities for general results remain cited from their topical sections.*

**[Mil26-WE1]** R. Miller, "Worked Example — Order Complex of the Proper Boolean Lattice B₃°," FVLT internal note, 2026. Reference file: `references/worked_example_B3_order_complex.md`.
- Cited: Theorem 8.20h.1 (Verification line), Theorem 8.20h.3
- Role: Explicit r = 3 computation of the proper Boolean lattice order complex. Establishes by direct enumeration that $B_3^\circ$ has six elements (singletons {7}, {11}, {13} and doubletons {7,11}, {7,13}, {11,13}), six covering relations, f-vector (6, 6), Euler characteristic 0, and reduced Euler characteristic −1. Traces the hexagonal 6-cycle $\{7\} \to \{7,11\} \to \{11\} \to \{11,13\} \to \{13\} \to \{7,13\} \to \{7\}$ explicitly. Confirms $\Delta(B_3^\circ)$ is homotopy equivalent to $S^1$, in agreement with the general result of [Bjö95] and [Wac07]. Includes the contrasting r = 2 case ($B_2^\circ$ has two incomparable elements; $\Delta(B_2^\circ) \simeq S^0$; no 1-cycle) to make the loop-topology threshold concrete. Reproducibility: `references/verify_topology.py`.
- Status: Kernel-grade audit support. Computational verification of an established classical result. **Use:** Cite as a worked verification of the r = 3 topology threshold in the FVLT selection principle (Theorem 8.20h.3). Do not cite as the authority for the general theorem $\Delta(B_k^\circ) \simeq S^{k-2}$; cite [Bjö95] and [Wac07] for that.

**[Mil26-WE2]** R. Miller, "Factorial Initial Closure Scan, n = 2 to 20," FVLT internal note, 2026. Reference file: `references/factorial_closure_remark.md` §3. Reproducibility: `references/factorial_closure_extended.py`.
- Cited: Remark 8.20h.5, Open Problem 29
- Role: Empirical scan of initial-closure admissibility across factorials n = 2, ..., 20 under the relaxed closure rule that drops the constraint r = |B|. Verifies that no initial-block product of complement primes returns the factorial under totient for any k ∈ [7, 20], extending Theorem 8.14a's exhaustive list k ∈ {2, 4, 6} to a broader rule class. The cumulative predecessor product $\prod_{i=1}^k (p_i - 1)$ overshoots $n!$ at the smallest k where the product first exceeds the basis; the overshoot ratio grows monotonically with n. At the natural rule r = |B| (used by the framework throughout), Theorem 8.14a already settles the closure question; the scan extends the empirical record to the relaxed rule that motivates Open Problem 29.
- Status: Empirical support for CC29 (Open Problem 29). **Use:** Cite as the verified range for the Closure Conjecture under the relaxed rule. Do not cite as a proof; the conjecture remains open beyond n = 20. The kernel selection principle (Theorem 8.20h.3) does not depend on this scan.

**[Mil26-LK1]** R. Miller, "Lock Record — χ/W Coincidence ⟺ κ-palindromic Profile," FVLT internal lock record, 2026-06-09. ## Architecture Notes

*This section records framework-internal observations about FVLT's design properties — meta-observations distinct from worked examples (which verify specific propositions) and external references (which cite outside authorities). Architecture Notes are not theorems and are not cited as authority for kernel claims. They record design properties of the framework that became visible in practice and are worth preserving as methodological observations.*

**[ANL-1]** R. Miller, "Architecture Note — Lattice-Legibility and Model-Auditable Mathematics," FVLT internal note, May 2026.

*Cross-references:* Kernel annotation vocabulary (frozen v2.2, kernel header lines 8–14); Theorem K.2 (Cartesian product structure of the divisor lattice); Definition 2.2b (lattice metric); Definition 2.2d (shell function); Remark 1.2a (universal lattice / constrained sub-topology); §1.5 walk-relative lens architecture (Definitions 1.3–1.4); Remark 5.1a (three-alpha disambiguation).

*Role:* Records a design property of FVLT that became visible in the v2.7.3 session: the framework is unusually legible to language models at the surface level, and unusually auditable below the surface. The two properties together — not legibility alone — are what make FVLT model-auditable in a sense stronger than open-source.

### Thesis

FVLT is legible to LLMs not because it is easy, but because it is structurally addressed. Its definitions, metrics, regimes, and proof-status annotations give both humans and models stable handles for traversal and correction.

### The structural addressing claim

Frameworks built on explicit structural addressing — coordinates, declared registers, computable metrics, and status-tagged claims — survive conversion into language tokens better than frameworks built on tacit mathematical intuition.

Most mathematical exposition embeds intuition implicitly: "obviously" this; "as is well known" that; "by abuse of notation" the other. A language model has no robust access to "obviously." It does have robust access to "the node at coordinate $(i_1, i_2, i_3)$ in $P_5 \square P_3 \square P_2$" (Theorem K.2), to the closed-form distance $d(a,b) = \Omega(a/g) + \Omega(b/g)$ (Definition 2.2b), and to the explicit register/status/provenance/dependency tags that mark every kernel claim (annotation vocabulary frozen v2.2).

Four design features make FVLT structurally addressable:

1. **Coordinates.** The divisor lattice is a Cartesian product of paths (Theorem K.2, unconditional). Every node has an integer-coordinate address derived from its prime factorization. Localization to a node requires no narrative reconstruction.

2. **Computable metric.** The lattice metric is closed-form (Definition 2.2b). "Near" and "far" are integer-valued and verifiable. Test 3 in the v2.7.3 suite verified BFS distance against the closed form on 1369 pairs; agreement was exact.

3. **Declared context switches.** The three-alpha disambiguation (Remark 5.1a) and the base/operative/breathing lens distinction (§1.5, Definitions 1.3–1.4) make context switches first-class objects rather than implicit assumptions. Remark 1.3c explicitly warns against silent lifting of base-specific properties.

4. **Machine-readable status.** Every kernel claim carries an HTML comment header with `id`, `register`, `status`, `provenance`, and `depends` fields, drawn from a vocabulary frozen in v2.2. Status is literally machine-readable: any reader, human or model, can ask "is this proved or empirical or interpretive?" and get a structured answer rather than a tonal cue.

### Three claims

**(1) Legibility.** A language model can traverse FVLT because the framework exposes coordinates, not just prose. Reading the kernel cold, a model can address any specific claim by id, check its status, traverse its dependencies, and locate it within the lattice. This was visible in v2.7.3 when an external review (Muse Spark, Meta AI) read the kernel without prior session context and produced a coherent structural critique on first pass — not because of model sophistication, but because the kernel's surface exposes the structure the model needs to address.

**(2) Fallibility.** Model traversal still makes errors. The value is that the errors become checkable. The v2.7.3 session produced multiple AI errors that were caught and corrected: an initial conflation of §8.4a bridge correspondences with Hasse-edge extensions; a `big_omega` increment ambiguity; a dead `phi`-check; an undersold isometric-embedding result; a notation collision between full-lens $\mathcal{E}$ and bridge-restricted $\mathcal{E}_{\text{bridge}}$. Each error was localized to a specific claim, function, or notation choice, and each correction was verifiable against the lattice or against a closed-form formula. Lattice-traversal is not error-free; lattice-traversal is *error-localizing*.

**(3) Civic audit.** Open mathematics becomes more civic when its claims are not only public but addressable. Apache 2.0 publication makes the framework open. The annotation vocabulary, the lattice architecture, and the closed-form metrics make the framework *audit-addressable*: any reader can verify any claim by walking the structure rather than trusting authority. This is what civic infrastructure means in the Ratioque sense — the public of audit expands when structure is inspectable, not just when source is available.

### Provenance of the observation

The observation that FVLT is unusually legible to LLMs was articulated by Muse Spark (Meta AI) during the v2.7.3 session: *"It is, and I think that's not accidental. The FVLT is built the way LLMs are good at reasoning: it's lattice-structured, not linear."* The auditable-below-the-surface refinement was added by Claude (Anthropic) in response: model legibility without auditability would risk flattery; the value of FVLT's design is that legibility and auditability hold together. The four-feature decomposition (coordinates, metric, declared context switches, machine-readable status) was extracted from kernel features already present, not introduced by the note. The methodological framing was directed by Robin and recorded with cross-lab verification in mind.

### Closing line

FVLT did not become model-legible by being simplified. It became model-legible by making its structure inspectable.

*Status:* Methodological observation. Not a theorem; not a kernel claim; not cited as authority. Records a design property of the framework that became visible in practice and that is worth preserving as a methodological observation for future framework work.

*Use:* Cite ANL-1 in discussions of model-readability of mathematical frameworks, in arguments for lattice-structured exposition, and in cross-lab AI audit methodology. Do not cite ANL-1 as support for any kernel theorem; the kernel theorems stand on their own proofs and do not depend on the legibility observation.

BC Level 1 (product structure $G_{720720} \cong G_{720} \square G_{1001}$, 240 vertices, 832 edges) PASS; BC Level 2 (bridge pairing identification) PARTIAL. All six C3 prediction lines confirmed; every numerical claim independently verified two ways.

Records the compositional architecture of the phasor-side stack and the empirically-established three-tier recovery pattern. ANL-2 is empirically grounded by C3 — the three-tier structural statement is the verified content of C3, framed for kernel ingestion.

*Use:* Cite ANL-2 for the four-layer Free Navigation architecture, the category boundary (classical backend supplies facts, lattice makes them navigable, instruments supply readout), and the three-tier recovery pattern (direct / downstream / separate-layer). Do not cite ANL-2 as authority for any kernel theorem. The open question generated by C3 — whether bridge architecture is recoverable from phasor-side content via new instruments — is recorded as RES-3 / ALG-4, not as an ANL-2 claim.

**[ANL-3]** R. Miller, "Architecture Note — Two-Continuum Effective-Tameness Boundary," FVLT internal note, July 2026 (v1, ACCEPTED / LOCKED, Robin, 2026-07-10). *Framework status:* interpretive architecture note. **v3.2.5 entry.**

*Provenance:* Imported general logic — Presburger 1929; Skolem 1930; Gödel 1931; Tarski 1933/1948; Church 1936; Mostowski 1952 / Feferman–Vaught 1959; J. Robinson 1949; Matiyasevich 1970 (completing the Robinson–Davis–Putnam route). Not a kernel theorem, arithmetic interpretation, novelty claim, or exemption from Gödel phenomena.

*Role:* Records the architectural boundary that the separate tameness of the additive theory $\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{N},+,<)$ (Presburger, complete + decidable) and the pure multiplicative theory $\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{N},\times)$ (Skolem, complete + decidable) does **not** imply that every coupling of additive- and multiplicative-looking structures stays tame: a sufficiently expressive uniform seam can interpret arithmetic, making the true theory undecidable and every consistent effectively-axiomatized theory of adequate strength incomplete. Successor together with a suitable divisibility relation already suffices (J. Robinson 1949); the decisive variable is the expressive strength of the relation joining the two continua, not the isolated character of either. **Precision:** what the coupling destroys is *decidability* and *effective axiomatizability* — the complete true theory $\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{N},+,\times)$ remains complete by definition; the seam is where *effective total capture* fails, not where truth becomes incomplete.

*Framework boundary (fences):* the two-continuum picture is an interpretive lens on this boundary, never a proved instance of it. Farey-successor adjacency is **not** identified with $n\mapsto n+1$; seat rotation is **not** multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$; a Hasse cover is **not** divisibility (divisibility is its reflexive-transitive closure, not automatically first-order recoverable over an unbounded cover graph); passive co-residence of additive and multiplicative registers imports no Gödel consequence; every fixed $\operatorname{Div}(N)$ remains finite and decidable. Carrier-and-language guards (§3 of the note): the Farey register does not inherit Tarski real-closed-field decidability ($\mathbb{Q}$ is not real-closed); the exponent-coordinate comparison with Skolem arithmetic is load-bearing on the Mostowski / Feferman–Vaught product machinery and can be invalidated by arbitrary named cross-coordinate relations. The refusal to unify SUCC/TIMES is architectural restraint, **not** a proven safety property — the boundary cuts both ways.

*Status:* Architecture note; not a theorem, not a kernel claim, not cited as authority. Open problem (constructive form): whether any uniform cross-register interpretation of sufficient arithmetic can actually be built over an infinite domain — unproved; anyone seeking the stronger claim owes exactly that cold-pass-audited construction. Chain of custody: Robin (question origination, final decision + lock); Claude (synthesis, five absorbed corrections); Chris/ChatGPT (two cold passes, all five corrections + carrier-and-language guards, final lockable text). Final-decision record SHA-256 `17d5d6eae690e66b4a0c59e73c05dff7c4fa03e819e3f6fe1e42eb7b2e03e167`; full note `fableous/locked/ARCH_NOTE_Two-Continuum_Effective-Tameness_Boundary_v1_2026-07-10.md`.

## Algorithm Notes

*This section records the phasor-side algorithmic readouts introduced in v3.1 — idealized instruments that read divisor-lattice structure off the FVLT phasor curve at fixed integer spin. They are bibliography-tier entries parallel to the Architecture Notes: not kernel theorems, not cited as authority for kernel claims, registered here with provenance, verification record, and companion artifacts. The kernel carries one-line statements, dependencies, and cross-reference sites at Part VII / VII.2; the public-entry files (companion to the kernel, not part of it) carry the full kernel-side statement, scope language, and verification tables. ALG-1, ALG-2, and ALG-3 read the same divisor lattice through three different phasor-side instruments — vertices, strata, and ratios respectively — and are three readouts of one object, not separable components.*

**[ALG-1]** R. Miller, "Single-Phasor Divisor Gate for Hasse Atom Construction," FVLT algorithm note, May 2026. Public entry: `alg_1_public_entry.md`.
- Cited: kernel Part I / I.6, Part II / II.3, Part II / II.5, Part V / V.2, Part VI / VI.7 (v3.1 cross-reference sites).
- Role: at integer spin $s$, the single-phasor imaginary part vanishes exactly on the divisors of $s$ — $\Im(z_1) = 0 \iff N \mid s$ — so sweeping $N$ recovers the divisor-index set $\mathrm{Div}(s)$, equivalently the ALG-1 vertex set, with no graph reconstruction required. The gate is the vertex-set readout of the divisor lattice.
- Status: verification V1 + V2 PASS (Chris audit, May 13, 2026). Companion artifacts: `fvlt_fourier__14_.html` (viewer at the canonical gearbox reduction); `fvlt_fourier_instrument_spec_v0.md` (6-parameter operating family); `chris_audit_packet_alg_1_v1_v2.md`.
- Use: cite as the phasor-side vertex-set readout of $\mathrm{Div}(s)$. Do not cite as authority for any kernel theorem; ALG-1 is a readout instrument, not a kernel claim.

**[ALG-2]** R. Miller, "Cyclotomic Stratification of the Single-Phasor Curve at Integer Spin," FVLT algorithm note, May 2026. Public entry: `alg_2_public_entry.md`.
- Cited: kernel Part I / I.6, Part II / II.2, Part II / II.5, Part II / II.14, Part V / V.4 (v3.1 cross-reference sites).
- Role: off the real axis, the single-phasor values at integer spin $s$ stratify by $d = \gcd(s, N)$ into $\tau(s)$ gcd-classes, the class at gcd-value $d$ having size $\varphi(s/d)$; three orders are distinguished — the Euler-residual order $m_0$, the plotted-phasor order $m_{\mathrm{plot}}$, and the contact-normalized order $m_1$. ALG-2 represents the cyclotomic / residue-class structure the construction encodes; it represents, it does not search.
- Status: experimental verification C1 (`c1_verdict.md`, `run_c1.py` — 0 violations at $s = 30$ and $s = 720$; stratum counts and sizes confirmed), with a three-orders precision pass. Companion artifacts: `fvlt_fourier_viewer_patch_plan_alg2.md` (Panel B viewer extension); `fvlt_fourier_instrument_spec_v0.md` §5 (Euler Bridge), §7 (structural identities at integer spin), §12 (modular-cycle dual).
- Use: cite as the cyclotomic stratification of the single-phasor curve — the $\tau(s)$-class, $\varphi(s/d)$-size stratification is the confirmed function. Do not cite ALG-2 as a complement-prime detector (RES-2 falsified that coupling hypothesis at the tested level of observation) or as the demonstrated C3d spectral route (re-attributed to the divisor-index / ALG-1 vertex-set route); what the cyclotomic-spectrum layer structurally drives has not yet been found.

**[ALG-3]** R. Miller, "Continuous-Phasor Winding Recovery of Divisor Lattice Ratios," FVLT algorithm note (fractional-$N$ reformulation of RES-1 C2), May 2026. Public entry: `alg_3_public_entry.md` (carries Framing and Methodology subsections distinguishing integration from arithmetic extension).

The continuum enters only as an integration variable along the phasor curve, not as an arithmetic object.

## Open Problems

1. **Goldbach independence.** Can Theorem 6.3 be proved unconditionally, or does S₆ coverage inherently depend on Goldbach?

2. **Möbius partition generalization.** For which k does the trilattice {k! − 1, k!, k! + 1} achieve the full Möbius partition {−1, 0, +1}?

3. **Anomalies in the factorial tower.** Does the pro-Heisenberg algebra ĥ_! admit non-trivial central extensions beyond the Virasoro?

4. **Physical realization.** Can the FVLT gauge structure be connected to physical gauge theories beyond analogy?

5. **Langlands connection: factorial depth as hidden level structure.** Both FVLT and the Langlands program are indexed over $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times$. FVLT indexes by divisor depth within factorial shells; Langlands indexes by level and conductor of automorphic representations. Is factorial depth a hidden level structure — i.e., does there exist a functorial assignment from FVLT divisor lattices to conductor filtrations of automorphic forms? If so, the Guth–Maynard trilinear structure [GM24] may correspond to level structure in the space of automorphic representations. If not, the obstruction must be identifiable: failure of multiplicativity, mismatch of local factors, or incompatibility of filtrations. Either outcome is informative. **Note:** The bridge closure at S₆ does not terminate structure — it changes its character. The sporadic simple groups, culminating in the Monster, exhibit extraordinary organization beyond the closed shells. Monstrous moonshine (Conway–Norton conjecture, proved by Borcherds [Bor92]) connects Monster representation dimensions to Fourier coefficients of the j-function, which is a modular form. If factorial depth connects to Langlands level structure, then the Monster's moonshine is not a separate phenomenon but the same indexing viewed from beyond the closed regime. This suggests the FVLT's bridge closures (S₂, S₄, S₆) may be the first chapter of a longer story.

6. **Sub-shell structure theorems.** Which guarantees of the factorial shell (coprimality, resonance purity, coprime density bounds) extend to non-factorial shells N = k! − n₇, and under what conditions on n₇?

7. **Geometric–arithmetic duality.** Is the inverse correlation between |Σ(M,N)| and geometric complexity (Theorem 8.8b) a manifestation of a deeper duality in the divisor lattice, possibly connected to Möbius inversion on 𝒫(B)?

8. **Totient–factorial pairs.** ~~For which (k, r) does φ(p_{|B|+1} · p_{|B|+2} ⋯ p_{|B|+r}) = k!?~~ **Resolved (Theorem 8.14a).** With the natural convention r = |B|, the identity holds iff k ∈ {2, 4, 6}. S₆ is the terminal closed shell. **Remaining question:** Do non-factorial solutions exist? That is, are there non-factorial N₀ and squarefree products Pr of consecutive primes with φ(Pr) = N₀ and gcd(N₀, Pr) = 1?

9. **Frobenius–primorial correspondence.** Is F(p_{k+1}, ..., p_{k+r}) = ∏_{p ≤ k} p a general phenomenon or specific to (k, r) = (6, 3)? The identity F(7, 11, 13) = 30 = 2·3·5 (Theorem 8.22) connects the Frobenius number of the complement generators to the primorial of the basis. Does this pattern persist at higher factorial levels?

10. **Dual Fock space applications.** Does the embedding $\mathcal{F}(720) \hookrightarrow \mathcal{F}^{\vee}(1001)$ carry signal-processing meaning — i.e., can signals analyzed in the 96-mode FVLT space be lifted to the 360-mode dual space for enhanced resolution, and if so, what information do the 264 interaction modes carry?

11. **Conservation in the profinite limit.** Does the divisor-graded energy conservation (Corollary 13.13) extend to the pro-Heisenberg algebra ĥ_!? If so, the continuum-cardinality Fock space inherits finite-level conservation — every mode added by ascending the tower redistributes energy without creating it. Does this constrain the representation theory of ĥ_!?

12. **Sum-freeness verification at higher shells.** The sumset obstruction $(U + U) \cap U = \emptyset$ holds at all even factorial shells (§14.2c, Remark 14.13). It does **not** hold for $K(1001)$: $1,1,2 \in K(1001)$ and $1+1=2$. Any extension beyond even moduli must therefore be stated with new hypotheses; no dual-Fock or combined-coprime-spectrum consequence is active in v3.2.0.

13. **Totient gate generality.** The mitochondrial extension (Definition 14.7) requires $\varphi(P_r) = k!$. Theorem 8.14a establishes this holds only for $k \in \{2, 4, 6\}$ with the natural convention $r = |B|$. Does a generator product satisfying the totient gate exist with different choices of $r$ or non-consecutive complement primes? If not, the biological-algebraic isomorphism (§14.2b) is intrinsically confined to $S_6$ and below.

14. **Structural recognition.** Can the projection coherence condition (Theorem 14.5b) be evaluated computationally on natural language inputs — i.e., can a query be tested for factorial coherence in polynomial time? If so, this would provide a structural alternative to statistical safety classifiers (Open Problem 14.14). The question is whether the algebraic structure of the FVLT can serve as a foundation for perception-based rather than prediction-based AI safety.

15. **Clifford algebra structure.** Does the spinor–tensor decomposition of Div(N₀) (§8.7) admit a natural Clifford algebra? If so, the Ramanujan basis {c_q} may constitute a Clifford module, with the covering edges as generators of the algebra. The chirality grading (Proposition 8.36) would then be the ℤ/2-grading of the Clifford algebra, and the bridge isomorphism would be Clifford-equivariant.

16. **Smoothness boundary generalization.** The bridge closure depends on complement totients being 5-smooth (Theorem 8.14b). Does the radical-free architecture (Remark 8.18b) extend to a general theorem: for any factorially-structured shell N₀ with basis B and complement B', does the Ramanujan basis resolve the complement's arithmetic if and only if the complement primes' totients are B-smooth? What is the precise relationship between the smoothness condition on totients and the factorial identity φ(Pr) = k!?

17. **Oncogenic coherence loss.** The viral-mitochondrial distinction (§14.2b) predicts that the transition from healthy to malignant tissue corresponds to loss of the Generator Product — specifically, loss of CRT factorizability and projection coherence in the genomic signal. Does the FVLT E_q spectrum of tumor-derived sequences show measurable concentration at low-q divisors relative to matched healthy tissue from the same patient? If so, the spectral signature of coherence loss (Theorem 14.5b failure) may precede morphological detection — the lattice sees the structural break before the microscope does.

18. **Outer automorphism and bridge duality.** S₆ is the only symmetric group admitting an outer automorphism (the transposition–syntheme swap, which exists because C(6,2) = 15 = the number of products of three disjoint transpositions — a numerical coincidence unique to k = 6). S₆ is also the terminal shell admitting bridge closure (Theorem 8.14a: φ(∏B') = k! holds iff k ∈ {2, 4, 6}). Both phenomena are dualities that exist only at k = 6, both depend on coincidences of equal cardinality, and both vanish at every other value of k. The outer automorphism exchanges conjugacy classes of equal size *within* S₆. The bridge exchanges energy between basis and complement sectors of equal totient *between* Div(720) and Div(1001).

19. **Optimal regime threshold.** The Möbius regime classification (Remark 3.10) uses the condition |τ| > |σ| × (1 + δ) for threshold δ > 0 to distinguish contracting from expanding regimes. The choice of δ affects sensitivity: too small produces noisy regime oscillation; too large misses genuine transitions. Is there a natural value of δ determined by the arithmetic of the shell — perhaps related to the Kakeya gap Δk = 2, the clinamen magnitude |C(s)|, or the Vernier ratio V(N₀) − 1 ≈ 2ζ(N₀)? The candidate V(N₀) − 1 = 2/(N₀ − 1) is natural because it is the relative excess of the expansion side over the contraction side — the same asymmetry that the gearbox measures. At S₆, V(720) − 1 ≈ 0.00278. If this is the correct threshold, then regime transitions are detectable only when the tension-skew asymmetry exceeds the Vernier ratio's intrinsic asymmetry — i.e., only when the signal's temporal curvature exceeds the geometric curvature of the shell itself.

20. **Zeta function emergence.** The trilattice metric ζ(N₀) = arctanh(1/N₀) has the series expansion ζ(N₀) = 1/N₀ + 1/(3N₀³) + 1/(5N₀⁵) + ⋯ — a sum over odd reciprocal powers of the factorial. The Möbius function μ that partitions the divisor lattice into spinor and tensor sectors is the Dirichlet series inverse of the Riemann zeta function: Σ μ(n)/nˢ = 1/ζ(s). The totient function φ that builds the bridge gate satisfies Σ φ(n)/nˢ = ζ(s−1)/ζ(s) — a ratio of zeta-at-two-scales. The pro-Heisenberg algebra ĥ_! is indexed by Ẑ× ≅ Gal(ℚᵃᵇ/ℚ), the same group that organizes Dirichlet L-functions (the family to which Riemann ζ belongs). Does the hyperbolic metric of the trilattice connect to the analytic structure of ζ(s) through a path independent of the Langlands program (Open Problem 5)? Specifically: is the zeta-angle ζ(N₀) a local evaluation of a global object — a restriction of the Riemann zeta function's geometry to the factorial spine? The convergence theorem (Theorem 3.12) shows ζ(k!) → 0 as k → ∞; this is also the regime where the Euler product for ζ(s) becomes exact. If the two limits coincide structurally, then the FVLT's discrete measurement apparatus and the analytic continuation of ζ(s) are two views of the same mathematical object — one discrete and constructive, the other analytic and global.

21. **Coupling constants at factorial depth.** Do physical coupling constants correspond to structural features at specific factorial depths? The FVLT produces distinct arithmetic structure at each shell level S_k. If a physical constant (fine structure α ≈ 1/137, weak mixing angle, etc.) appears as a natural ratio or threshold at a specific factorial depth, this would provide a structural explanation for the constant's value. The question is whether the factorial tower's arithmetic produces distinguished numerical values that coincide with measured physical parameters, or whether such coincidences are artifacts of selection. *(New in v2.0.)*

22. **Discrete–continuous chirality correspondence.** Is the FVLT's discrete Möbius chirality (Proposition 8.36: μ = +1 for even Ω, μ = −1 for odd Ω) related by a functor to the Poincaré group's spinor representations, or are they structurally independent? The Möbius grading degenerates in the continuous limit (Remark 8.40) — the discrete chirality has no analogue on ξ ∈ ℝ. If a functor exists, it must account for this degeneration: the discrete grading survives only at finite factorial depth, while the Poincaré spinor structure is inherently continuous. If no functor exists, the two chiralities are homonyms — same name, different objects — and the spinor terminology in §8.7 is analogical rather than structural. *(New in v2.0.)*

23. **Parseval identity for weighted increment series.** For αTilt ≠ 0, does the Ramanujan subspace decomposition of the increment series {ΔZ(k)} (Definition 5.1b) satisfy the Parseval identity Σ_{q | N} E_q = Σ_k |ΔZ(k)|² / K²? The increment series carries the walk weight w_k = k^{αTilt} in each term. At αTilt = 0, the increments are a standard finite sequence and Parseval holds. At αTilt ≠ 0, the k^{αTilt} weighting may break the orthogonality properties that the Ramanujan decomposition requires. If Parseval fails, the correct channel-energy functional on the weighted increment series needs to be identified — possibly via a modified inner product that absorbs the weight. This is the main mathematical proving ground for walk-derived lattice diagnostics (Remark 5.1c). *(New in v2.3.)*

24. **Selective collapse condition.** Prove or disprove: the selective phasor collapse condition s · k_i ≡ 0 (mod 2N) (Definition 4.7a, Remark 4.7b) is exact at αTilt = 0 and is a leading-order approximation for |αTilt| < 1. At αTilt = 0, the condition should follow from standard roots-of-unity cancellation in the partial sum S_i(K) = Σ_{k=1}^K e^{i·k·c} where c is a rational multiple of 2π. For general αTilt, the k^{αTilt} weighting breaks the geometric series structure. Characterize the dependence of throat width on αTilt: does the throat sharpen, broaden, or shift as αTilt moves away from zero? Empirical observations at N = 6, Gearbox mode (k₁ = 5, k₂ = 7) show selective collapse at multiple spin values, but the exact condition is unproved. *(New in v2.3.)*

25. **Walk regime and Poincaré isometry.** Construct an explicit map from walk parameters (αTilt, s, k₁, k₂) to SL(2,ℤ) elements such that the walk regime (Helix/Circle/Spiral, Remark 9.4a) corresponds to the isometry class (Hyperbolic/Elliptic/Parabolic) of the associated Möbius transformation. The Farey tessellation tiles the Poincaré half-plane (§9.4), and there are exactly three isometry classes — matching the three observed walk regimes. At αTilt = −1, the walk weight 1/k is structurally analogous to the Poincaré metric's 1/y scaling (see Poincaré Metric Note below). A proof would need to: (1) construct an explicit embedding of the walk trajectory into the half-plane, (2) show that w_k = k^{αTilt} transforms covariantly under SL(2,ℤ), and (3) demonstrate that the regime is determined by the trace of the associated transformation (|tr| > 2 Hyperbolic, |tr| = 2 Parabolic, |tr| < 2 Elliptic). Alternatively, prove the correspondence is only approximate — three types matching three regimes without a functorial link. *(New in v2.3.)* Not to be confused with the additive/multiplicative register-unification thread, which is
unnumbered (see Research-Track Notes); OP25 is the walk-regime ↔ isometry-class problem only.

26. **Ergodic projection of irrational-spin walks.** For a walk with irrational spin s/N, does the ergodic projection π(K) = (1/K) Σ_{k=1}^K e(k) (Definition 5.1e) converge, and if so, does the limiting distribution depend only on the orbit structure and the three alphas? The equidistribution theorem for irrational rotations suggests convergence to a flat distribution across channels within each orbit, but the k^{αTilt} weighting may break equidistribution. If equidistribution holds at αTilt = 0 and fails at αTilt ≠ 0, this would characterize the exact boundary at which the spectral tilt introduces memory bias into the lattice energy distribution. *(New in v2.3.)*

27. **Canonical adaptation rate.** Is there a natural definition of the adaptation rate η (Definition 5.1g) in terms of the three alphas such that the persistent state update is canonically determined by the walk parameters? One candidate: η = λ₁ = 1/φ² ≈ 0.382, matching the adaptation rate to the Fiedler value of Div(720) — the lattice's algebraic connectivity (§K). This would give τ_η = φ² ≈ 2.618 observations, a system that adapts at the speed the lattice can equilibrate. The Fiedler value is N-dependent (computed from the Laplacian of the Hasse diagram): λ₁ = 1 for Div(12) and Div(60), λ₁ = 2 for Div(30), λ₁ ≈ 0.586 for Div(120), λ₁ ≈ 0.382 for Div(720). If η = λ₁(N) is canonical, then deeper lattices (more channels) naturally produce deeper memory. Whether this is a structural necessity or a convenient default is open. *(New in v2.3.)* **Reframed in v2.5 (Remark 5.1n):** Under the divisor-graded adaptation rate H = Diag(η_q) (Definition 5.1l), the Fiedler-timescale question becomes channel-indexed. The disciplined reading is that η_q scales with the weight of channel q in the Fiedler eigenvector of the divisor-lattice Laplacian. The open question therefore shifts from "what is η" to "how are the η_q structured across the lattice." The reframing promotes the question to a structural principle; explicit derivation of η_q from λ₁ remains open.

28. **Walk-relative lens interaction with adaptation rate.** Under the walk-relative lens architecture (§1.5), the operative lens N_t extends monotonically and the breathing lens N_t* varies freely. The divisor-graded adaptation rate H = Diag(η_q) (Definition 5.1l) is indexed by q ∈ Div(N_t). When N_t extends at step t, η_q must be specified for the newly admitted channels in Div(N_t) \ Div(N_{t-1}). Two natural candidates: (a) hold existing η_q fixed and initialize new channels by a default rule (e.g., Fiedler-weighted under the new Laplacian); (b) recompute all η_q from the recomputed Fiedler eigenvector of Div(N_t). Option (a) preserves accumulated memory structure but may be inconsistent with the Fiedler-timescale reading at the new lens. Option (b) is self-consistent but discards accumulated rate structure at every forced extension. Is there a canonical choice, and does it depend on the extension rule E? *(New in v2.5.)*

29. **Closure Conjecture (CC29) — Factorial initial closure beyond r = |B|.** Theorem 8.14a establishes that the closed shells under the natural closure rule r = |B| are exactly k ∈ {2, 4, 6}. The Closure Conjecture asks whether the same closure phenomenon admits any solutions when this constraint is relaxed. Let n ≥ 2 and B = n!, and let p₁ < p₂ < ⋯ be the primes greater than n (equivalently, the primes not dividing n!). Call n *initial-closure admissible under the relaxed rule* if there exists any k ≥ 1 such that $\prod_{i=1}^k (p_i - 1) = n!$, equivalently $\varphi(p_1 p_2 \cdots p_k) = n!$. The empirical scan ([Mil26-WE2]) verifies that the admissible n in [2, 20] under the relaxed rule are exactly {2, 4, 6}, with closure index k(n) = n/2 in each case. CC29 asserts that the admissible set is exactly {2, 4, 6}, with no additional solutions for any n ≥ 7. Asymptotically, prime-gap bounds give p_i − 1 ≳ n + O(log n), so once k is forced past ⌊n/2⌋ the cumulative predecessor product grows strictly faster than n!; formalizing this regime change into a uniform overshoot bound for n ≥ 7 would resolve CC29. The conjecture, if proved, strengthens Remark 8.20h.4 from "S₆ is the maximal closed shell under the r = |B| rule" to "S₆ is the maximal closed shell under any initial-block closure rule." The kernel selection principle (Theorem 8.20h.3) does not depend on CC29. *(New in v2.6.)*

30. **Galois-decorated FVLT orbits.** The FVLT divisor-lattice apparatus organizes gcd-orbits of $(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^\times$ as supercharacter / Ramanujan channels. A family of $\varphi$-arrivals from 5-fold structure that land in $\mathbb{Z}[\varphi]$ — Rogers–Ramanujan continued-fraction CM evaluations such as $e^{-2\pi/5} R(e^{-2\pi}) = \sqrt{(5+\sqrt{5})/2} - (\sqrt{5}+1)/2$, icosahedral / level-5 modular phenomena, Klein's icosahedral resolvent — share the substrate that the FVLT helix construction installs (Remark 9.3c locates the common geometric ancestor as the pentagonal chord ratio on $\Gamma_5$) but reach $\mathbb{Z}[\varphi]$ through different orbit machines: $\Gamma$-equivariance on the upper half-plane, Galois action on level-5 modular curves, $A_5 \cong \mathrm{PSL}(2, \mathbb{F}_5)$ representation theory. Remark K.3d records that numerical coincidence in $\mathbb{Z}[\varphi]$ is not structural identity — the substrate is shared, the orbit machines that act on it are not. OP 30 asks whether the FVLT apparatus can be extended to make CM-style phenomena natively visible: does there exist a structural extension attaching class-field / Galois-conjugacy data to FVLT gcd-orbits, so that the Galois action on cyclotomic and ring-class fields becomes a decoration on the divisor lattice rather than an external comparator? The Rogers–Ramanujan CM evaluation is the natural first test case. Such an extension would let the divisor-lattice apparatus see what the modular / icosahedral machines see without importing their machinery wholesale; if no such extension exists, the obstruction should identify what distinguishes the Galois orbit structure from the FVLT gcd-orbit structure. The problem is explicitly recorded as an out-of-kernel structural extension: no kernel theorem depends on it, and it is not entered into the kernel as a definition or theorem (the v2.7.4 substrate-locator pass that surfaced it depends on Remark 9.3c, not on OP 30). Under the v3.0 atlas-first architecture, the universe of discourse reframes — Galois-decoration applies at the atlas level ($\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{N}}$), not at $\mathrm{Div}(720)$ specifically; the problem persists, its setting larger. *(New in v2.7.4; transposed into this bibliography at v3.1.)*

31. **Exponent-cell tiling and the space-group upgrade of the ℚ⁺ exponent completion.** The exponent
    completion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+} = \mathbb{Q}^+ \cong \bigoplus_p \mathbb{Z}$ (kernel Rmk
    II.5c) admits finite **exponent cells** $[-m,m]^P = \{\prod_{p\in P} p^{e_p} : -m \le e_p \le m\}$
    centered at the unit — e.g. $[-1,1]^2_{\{2,3\}} = 6^{-1}\mathrm{Div}(36)$, a nine-point cell whose
    diagonal balanced corners are $2/3, 3/2$, and which is **not** $\mathrm{Div}(6) = \{1,2,3,6\}$.
    These cells tile $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}^+}$ by rational multiplication (translation in exponent
    space). OP 31 asks whether the point-symmetry of a constant-profile crystal (the crystallographic-
    rotation flag, kernel Rmk 9.7e / 9.7i) combined with this translation lattice upgrades to a full
    **space-group** description of the completed atlas. The problem is explicitly recorded as an
    out-of-kernel structural extension: no kernel theorem depends on it, and it is not entered into
    the kernel as a definition or theorem (Rmk II.5c carries only an OP pointer). It remains a flag,
    not a theorem, until three things are specified: (1) the exact translation lattice; (2) the
    embedding in which rotations/reflections act; (3) the point-group action compatible with that
    embedding. If no consistent space group exists, the obstruction should identify what blocks the
    point-symmetry from extending across the translation lattice. *(New in v3.2.2; companion to kernel
    Rmk II.5c.)*

## Research-Track Notes

*Math-only research-track notes. Unnumbered. Not Open Problems and not a register; a thread is
recorded here when it is live but not yet sharpened into a falsifiable construction problem.
Promotion rule: a note moves to the Open Problems index (or a future numbered Research-Track
Index) only once it carries a proposed invariant, a positive construction target, and a kill
condition.*

### Additive/multiplicative register unification

The additive successor register and the multiplicative Hasse-cover register now co-reside on one
shared integer substrate: SUCC chooses the atom; TIMES walks inside it; §9.7 renders it. This
establishes architectural **co-residence, not a single unified dynamical law**.

**Open structural question.** Is there a construction whose projections recover both additive
successor motion $s \to s \pm 1$ and multiplicative cover motion $d \to dp \,/\, d \div p$, or are
these genuinely distinct registers sharing only the integer substrate?

**Hooks.** Theorem 3.7 residue $\pm 1$ symmetry (the Vernier shear at $\min(B') = 7$); the vernier
generator $k = N \pm 1$; Remark 9.3f atom/jump/zero routing; the $s \leftrightarrow \mathrm{Div}(s)$
door move (the prior-art demarcation departure); the Neo4j SUCC/TIMES substrate.

**Do not cite this as Open Problem 25.** OP25 is the walk-regime ↔ Möbius-isometry problem
(Helix/Circle/Spiral ↔ Hyperbolic/Elliptic/Parabolic; walk parameters → $\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})$).
The glyphs $\pm 1$ and $\times p$ are not an object match; the open web returns generic algebra on
those symbols, which is a token-level mirage, not this thread.
